# The Center for Active Living (CAL): Comprehensive Municipal, Environmental, and Legislative Investigation

<!-- v1 – Definitive comprehensive investigation and historical analysis of Hingham's proposed municipal Center for Active Living (CAL) at Bare Cove Park Drive, documenting its origins, feasibility studies, environmental/Article 97 legislative proceedings, cost modeling, Town Meeting debate, and defeat under 2026 Warrant Article 12, with rigorous comparison to 100 Beal Street Senior Affordable Housing. -->

**Document Identifier:** `Center_for_Active_Living_CAL_Comprehensive_Report_v1.md`  
**Publication Date:** September 2026  
**Subject Entity:** Center for Active Living (CAL / HCAL)  
**Sponsoring Municipality:** Town of Hingham, Massachusetts (Select Board & Center for Active Living Building Committee)  
**Proposed Siting:** Bare Cove Park Drive, Hingham, MA 02043 (Assessor Map 69, Parcel 44; 0 Fort Hill Street)  
**Statutory Authorization Addressed:** 2026 Annual Town Meeting Warrant Article 12 ($29.93M Debt-Exclusion Borrowing)  
**Legislative Disposition:** **FAILED** at Annual Town Meeting on April 27, 2026 (510 In Favor vs. 470 Opposed; 52.0% achieved vs. 66.67% required supermajority under M.G.L. c. 44, § 7)  

---

## 1. Executive Summary

For over fifteen years, the Town of Hingham sought to resolve an acute public infrastructure deficit: providing an adequate, modern community center for its rapidly expanding older adult population. Since 1997, Hingham's senior center operations (administered by the Department of Elder Services / Council on Aging) have been housed in a cramped, windowless 5,200-square-foot basement space in Hingham Town Hall (210 Central Street).

The town's multi-year effort culminated in the proposal to construct a state-of-the-art, 25,950-square-foot **Center for Active Living (CAL)** on a 5.387-acre municipal site off **Bare Cove Park Drive** (Assessor Map 69, Parcel 44), adjacent to Bare Cove Park. The proposed development called for demolishing a blighted World War II naval munitions bunker, remediating localized soils, and constructing an all-electric, energy-efficient facility with 140 parking spaces at an estimated total cost of **$29,927,423**.

At the Hingham Annual Town Meeting on **April 27, 2026**, **Article 12** came before the town's legislative body requesting authorization to borrow up to $29,930,000 via a Proposition 2½ debt exclusion. Following more than two hours of intense, emotional floor debate at the Hingham High School gymnasium, voters cast their ballots:

$$	ext{Vote Tally: } 510 	ext{ In Favor (Yes)} \quad 	ext{vs.} \quad 470 	ext{ Opposed (No)}$$

While the motion secured a simple majority of **52.0%**, Massachusetts General Laws (M.G.L. c. 44, § 7) and municipal finance statutes mandate a strict **two-thirds (2/3) supermajority (66.67%)** for bonded municipal debt obligations. With 980 total votes cast, the measure required 654 affirmative votes to pass; it fell short by 144 votes. The Town Moderator declared the motion lost, abruptly terminating the Bare Cove Park Drive project and rendering the scheduled May 2, 2026 town-wide ballot question moot.

### Critical Demarcation: CAL vs. 100 Beal Street Senior Affordable Housing
A persistent source of public confusion during the 2025–2026 civic debates was the conflation of the Center for Active Living with the **100 Beal Street Senior Affordable Housing** project. These two initiatives are legally, structurally, and functionally distinct:

1. **Center for Active Living (CAL):** A **municipal public services facility** (daytime recreational, educational, nutritional, and health programs for town elders) proposed, funded, and managed directly by the **Town of Hingham municipal government** (Select Board and Council on Aging) on Bare Cove Park Drive.
2. **100 Beal Street:** A **residential apartment community** (68 affordable rental units for seniors aged 62+) sponsored by the **Hingham Housing Authority (HHA)**—an independent public body corporate and politic under M.G.L. c. 121B—in partnership with private developer **Peabody Properties** on School Tract II off Beal Street.

The Town Meeting defeat of Article 12 did not involve or invalidate 100 Beal Street, nor was CAL ever part of an authorized "land swap" with School Tract II. However, the political defeat of CAL significantly altered the local environmental landscape, redirecting organized civic opposition directly toward 100 Beal Street.

---

## 2. Demographic Imperative & Historical Origins (1997–2023)

### 2.1 The Town Hall Basement Facility (1997–Present)
In 1997, the Town of Hingham relocated its senior center into the lower ground level of Town Hall at 210 Central Street. The allotted footprint—approximately 5,200 square feet—was acknowledged as a temporary compromise. For nearly three decades, municipal senior services operated within this constrained envelope:
* **Spatial Deficit:** The facility lacked dedicated classrooms, exercise space, adequate staff offices, confidential counseling consultation rooms, and commercial-grade nutrition infrastructure.
* **Accessibility Challenges:** Located in a sub-grade basement level, mobility-impaired seniors faced awkward elevator transfers, poor interior daylighting, and difficult access from exterior parking areas.
* **Parking Bottlenecks:** The Town Hall campus hosts the Select Board, Town Clerk, Planning Board, Building Department, School Administration, and Central Fire Station. During municipal business hours and public hearings, senior center participants routinely faced zero available parking stalls.

### 2.2 UMass Gerontology Demographic Research
Hingham's senior demographics underwent dramatic expansion over the subsequent quarter-century. The town commissioned the **UMass Boston Gerontology Institute** to conduct rigorous demographic evaluations:

* **2013 Study (*Aging in Hingham: A Community Needs Assessment*):** Documented that Hingham's older adult population was outpacing regional and state averages, warning that existing municipal senior services would become severely overwhelmed without major capital expansion.
* **2022 Comprehensive Re-Assessment (*Aging in Hingham: Community Needs Assessment*):** 
  * Revealed that 34% of Hingham seniors lived alone, elevating risks of social isolation, cognitive decline, and depression.
  * Identified that 30% of local seniors were active caregivers for spouses or family members, requiring respite programming.
  * Documented that **32% of survey respondents routinely traveled to neighboring municipalities** (notably Marshfield, Hanover, and Scituate) to access senior programming and wellness services unavailable in Hingham.
* **May 2025 Study (*Healthy Aging in Massachusetts*):** Validated that compared to peer South Shore benchmark communities, Hingham elders (aged 65+) suffered from a higher-than-average incidence of multiple chronic medical conditions, underscoring the urgent need for local wellness, fitness, and nutritional infrastructure.
* **2025 Town Census & Long-Term Projections:**
  * **2025 Baseline:** 7,904 residents aged 60+, representing **32% of Hingham's total population**.
  * **2035 Projection:** Approximately 9,000 residents aged 60+, climbing to **39% of the town's population**—positioning Hingham as having the highest percentage of seniors among all South Shore peer municipalities.

---

## 3. Site Selection & Feasibility Analysis (2020–2025)

### 3.1 Siting Mandate & Committee Evolution
Recognizing that Town Hall senior operations were untenable, the **2020 Annual Town Meeting (Articles 14 & 15)** appropriated $525,000 for architectural design/engineering and formally established the **Senior Center Building Committee (SCBC)**.

In 2023, the town recognized that confining the search strictly to Town Hall was unworkable. **Article 15 of the 2023 Annual Town Meeting** amended the original 2020 warrant to authorize the evaluation of alternative town-owned parcels across Hingham and officially renamed the body the **Center for Active Living Building Committee (CALBC)**.

### 3.2 Evaluation of Alternative Sites
Between 2021 and early 2026, the CALBC held **67 public meetings**, collaborating with specialized consultants:
* **Architectural Firm of Record:** EDM Studio, Inc.
* **Civil & Environmental Engineering:** SLR International Corporation
* **Senior Facility Specialist:** Lifespan Design Studio

The committee conducted rigorous comparative analyses across numerous prospective sites:

| Prospective Location | Municipal Analysis & Findings | Siting Determination |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Town Hall Expansion (4 Configurations)** | Examined occupying the vacated Police Department wing (following the police department's move to 335 Lincoln Street) and building structural additions. | **Rejected:** Building layout could not provide sufficient contiguous square footage. Severe structural limitations; high cost per sq. ft.; expansion would require paving over the adjacent **Cronin or Haley athletic fields** for parking, triggering fierce community backlash. Remaining space subsequently pursued by South Shore Regional Emergency Communications Center (SSRECC). |
| **South Shore Country Club (Union St)** | Evaluated utilizing driving range acreage off Union Street. | **Rejected:** Proximity to Hingham High School caused severe traffic gridlock; incompatible recreation master plan; extensive environmental constraints. |
| **Hitchcock Shoes Site (225 Beal St)** | Evaluated acquiring the private commercial parcel and parking facility. | **Rejected:** Privately held property under active purchase-and-sale agreement; potential commercial acquisition costs and site configuration constraints. |
| **Plymouth River School (High St)** | Evaluated utilizing vacant school-district acreage or administrative building space. | **Rejected:** Hingham School Department formally determined that the campus was fully required for continuous educational functions. (Land was later utilized as Article 97 conservation replacement land). |
| **100 Beal Street (School Tract II)** | Examined town-owned land along Beal Street. | **Excluded:** The ~15-acre parent parcel (Map 58, Lot 23) was owned in fee simple by the **Hingham Housing Authority (HHA)** under statutory deed restriction (Book 51379, Page 244) and formally committed under state procurement laws (M.G.L. c. 30B) for senior affordable rental housing. |
| **Bare Cove Park Drive (Map 69, Lot 44)** | Evaluated a 5.387-acre municipal footprint containing an abandoned, blighted World War II Naval Ammunition Depot munitions building (0 Fort Hill Street). | **RECOMMENDED & SELECTED:** Sufficient upland area for single-story 25,950-sq. ft. footprint, 140 parking stalls, natural wooded setting, town ownership, and opportunity to remediate a municipal brownfield site. |

---

## 4. Engineering, Architectural, & Siting Specifications

### 4.1 Building Program & Specialized Code Compliance
The design prepared by EDM Studio and Lifespan Design Studio established a regional benchmark for modern senior services:
* **Gross Floor Area:** **25,950 square feet**, single-story construction, ensuring universal ADA barrier-free access.
* **Energy Architecture:** Fully electric, zero-fossil-fuel building envelope designed under Massachusetts **Specialized Energy Code** standards, incorporating high-performance insulation, heat pumps, and photovoltaic rooftop solar readiness.
* **Parking Capacity:** **140 surface parking spaces** with EV charging stations and dedicated van/minibus passenger loading canopies.
* **Brownfield Remediation:** Demolition and hazardous materials abatement of an abandoned, reinforced-concrete WWII Naval Ammunition Depot ordnance storage bunker, accompanied by extensive soil stabilization ($6M–$8M estimated site preparation component).
* **Landscape & Stormwater:** Advanced Low-Impact Development (LID) stormwater infiltration basins and planting of **250 native trees and conservation shrubs**.

### 4.2 Comprehensive Senior Service Delivery Model
Modeled on the highly successful Marshfield Senior Center (expanded to 24,000 sq. ft. in 2022, where 55% of town seniors participate annually), the Hingham CAL program encompassed:
1. **Health & Wellness:** Clinical examination rooms for blood pressure and influenza immunization clinics; private consultation suites for SHINE (Serving the Health Insurance Needs of Elders) Medicare counseling; specialized fitness center with impact-absorbing flooring for mobility and balance therapy.
2. **Nutrition & Food Security:** Commercial-grade commercial kitchen supporting daily congregate senior lunches, culinary/nutrition classes, community café for informal socialization, and regional dispatch center for Meals on Wheels.
3. **Lifelong Learning & Arts:** Multi-purpose classrooms hosting Bridgewater Senior University courses, civic lectures, clean/wet arts and crafts studios, and digital technology training labs.
4. **Caregiver & Social Support:** Professional social worker offices supporting family caregivers, Alzheimer's/dementia support groups, fuel assistance counseling, and property tax work-off coordination.
5. **Municipal Transportation Hub:** Dispatch and maintenance depot for town senior vans providing local medical appointment transport, grocery transit, and regional day trips.

---

## 5. Environmental, Wetlands, & Article 97 Legislative Journey

### 5.1 The Article 97 Constitutional Transfer Process
Because the 5.387-acre site off Bare Cove Park Drive was designated as public park and recreational land, conversion to municipal building use triggered **Article 97 of the Amendments to the Massachusetts Constitution**, which strictly prohibits transferring or changing the use of protected public parkland without a two-thirds vote of the Legislature:

1. **Town Meeting Authorization (ATM 2025, Article 16):** Hingham Town Meeting approved by a two-thirds majority a petition authorizing the Select Board to file special state legislation to change the use of the 5.387 acres off Bare Cove Park Drive.
2. **Statutory Replacement Land:** In compliance with the state's **Public Lands Preservation Act (PLPA)**, Hingham designated approximately **7.0 acres of town-owned pristine forested land off High Street near Plymouth River School** to be transferred into the care, custody, and control of the Hingham Conservation Commission under permanent Article 97 protection.
3. **EOEEA Environmental Determination (October 3, 2025):** The Secretary of the Massachusetts Executive Office of Energy and Environmental Affairs (EOEEA) issued a formal determination finding that the replacement parcel off High Street provided equal or greater natural resource and water-supply protection value, noting that the High Street land sits entirely within high-yield aquifer recharge zones.
4. **Legislative Enactment (February 6, 2026):** The Massachusetts General Court enacted the special act into law, clearing the state constitutional hurdle for municipal construction.

### 5.2 Conservation Commission & Wetlands Permitting
The project underwent intensive environmental review before the Hingham Conservation Commission:
* **Applicant:** EDM Studio, Inc. / Town of Hingham
* **Environmental Engineer:** SLR International Corporation
* **Independent Peer Reviewer:** Horsley Witten Group, Inc. (HW)
* **Conservation Review:** Request for Determination of Applicability (RDA) and Order of Resource Area Determination (ORAD MassDEP File No. 034-1509).
* **Resource Areas Delineated:**
  * **Wetland A:** Bordering Vegetated Wetland (BVW) along the eastern property line, draining toward Hockley Run and Tucker's Swamp.
  * **Wetland B:** Isolated Vegetated Wetland (IVW) along the northern boundary.
  * **Potential Vernal Pool (PVP #10722):** Located within the northern wetland buffer.
  * **Perennial Stream:** MassDEP-confirmed perennial watercourse tributary to the **Weymouth Back River Area of Critical Environmental Concern (ACEC)**.
* **Permitting Status:** By early 2026, the CAL project had successfully secured regulatory approvals from the Hingham Conservation Commission, Planning Board, and Zoning Board of Appeals (ZBA).

---

## 6. Financial Architecture, Debt Exclusion, & Tax Impact

### 6.1 Construction Cost Capital Breakdown
As presented to the Advisory Committee and codified in the 2026 Town Meeting Warrant, the January 2026 total capital expenditure estimate was **$29,927,423** (rounded to $29.93M):

$$egin{array}{|l|r|r|}
\hline
	extbf{Capital Cost Component} & 	extbf{Expenditure Amount} & 	extbf{Percentage} \
\hline
	ext{Direct Building Construction} & \$19,964,549 & 66.7\% \
	ext{Site Development, Utilities \& Demolition} & \$6,213,950 & 20.8\% \
	ext{Soft Costs, Furnishings, Testing \& Contingencies} & \$4,348,924 & 14.5\% \
\hline
	extbf{Total Bonded Construction Borrowing} & \mathbf{\$29,927,423} & \mathbf{100.0\%} \
\hline
\end{array}$$

### 6.2 Ancillary Capital Improvements (Outside Construction Borrowing)
In addition to the $29.93M construction bond, the municipal plan leveraged existing capital accounts for surrounding infrastructure:
* **Water Infrastructure ($400,000):** Extension of a municipal water main along Bare Cove Park Drive to serve the building, park bubblers, and a new fire hydrant (funded via Department of Public Works capital budget).
* **Roadway Reconstruction ($275,000):** Full reclamation and repaving of Bare Cove Park Drive following utility trenching (funded via DPW budget).
* **Traffic Signalization ($300,000):** Proposed intersection signal at Fort Hill Street and Bare Cove Park Drive to enhance pedestrian and transit access (funded via Massachusetts Complete Streets, MassDOT, and Chapter 90 funds).

### 6.3 Operating Budget Projections
* **Baseline Budget (FY27):** $451,800, funding 4 full-time and 4 half-time staff members.
* **Incremental CAL Facility Costs:** Projected increase of **$225,000** in Year 1 for dedicated custodial maintenance, utilities, and commercial property insurance.
* **Revenue Offsets:** Supported by the Center for Active Living Revolving Fund (capped at $80,000 under 2026 Warrant Article 13), state formula grants, program activity fees, and charitable contributions from the newly established Friends of CAL.

### 6.4 Property Tax Impact Modeling (28-Year Bond Repayment)
The project proposed issuing Bond Anticipation Notes (BANs) of $15M in FY27 and $30M in FY28, transitioning to 28-year level principal general obligation bonds. 

The Select Board and Advisory Committee calculated that CAL borrowing would add an incremental **1.8% to local property taxes over five years** (averaging ~0.4% annually) above baseline municipal growth:

| Property Assessed Valuation | FY27 Tax Impact | FY28 Tax Impact | FY29 Peak Impact | FY30 Tax Impact | FY31 Tax Impact |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| **$750,000** | $30 | $60 | **$155** | $152 | $149 |
| **$1,075,000** *(Median Hingham Home)* | $43 | $86 | **$222** | $218 | $214 |
| **$1,367,000** *(Average Hingham Home)* | $55 | $109 | **$283** | $277 | $272 |
| **$2,000,000** | $80 | $160 | **$414** | $406 | $398 |
| **$2,500,000** | $100 | $200 | **$517** | $507 | $497 |

---

## 7. The Great Civic Debate: Bare Cove Park Under Scrutiny

As the April 2026 Town Meeting approached, the CAL proposal triggered one of the most passionate and polarized civic debates in Hingham's modern history.

### 7.1 Proponent Arguments & Municipal Coalition
The proponent coalition—anchored by the **Select Board (voted 3–0 in favor)**, a majority of the **Advisory Committee (voted 9–5 in favor)**, the **Council on Aging**, and the **CALBC**—framed the project as an essential, overdue moral obligation:
* **Generational Equity:** Seniors contributed to Hingham's tax base for decades, supporting multi-million-dollar school projects (including the recent $113M Foster Elementary School). It was unconscionable to relegate nearly 40% of the town's population to a windowless basement.
* **Public Health Necessity:** A modern senior facility is critical preventative healthcare infrastructure. Isolation, lack of mobility, and poor nutrition directly accelerate nursing home placement and municipal emergency services (EMS) calls.
* **Environmental Reclamation:** The proposed site was not a pristine virgin grove; it was a blighted, contaminated World War II munitions depot. Developing CAL would remediate asbestos, lead, and ordnance residues while adding 250 native trees.
* **Financial Prudence:** Sunk design costs exceeded $3 million. Delaying the project would expose the town to relentless construction inflation, guaranteeing that any future facility would cost substantially more.

### 7.2 Opponent Mobilization & Key Objections
The opposition coalition—led by the **Bare Cove Park Committee**, conservation organizations, neighborhood abutters along Beal and Fort Hill Streets, and a fiscal conservative bloc—mounted an aggressive campaign urging a "NO" vote:
* **Ecological Integrity of Bare Cove Park:** Opponents argued that Bare Cove Park is Hingham's premier natural jewel and wildlife refuge. Constructing a 26,000-sq. ft. building, 140 parking stalls, and lighting would permanently fragment contiguous wildlife corridors.
* **Cumulative Environmental Desiccation ("Death by a Thousand Cuts"):** Prominent environmentalists, including Mary Anne Jackson and Anita Bapooji Ryan, warned of an ecological catastrophe. Three concurrent projects encircled the sensitive wetlands:
  1. **Center for Active Living:** 5.387 acres on Bare Cove Park Drive;
  2. **100 Beal Street Senior Housing:** 8.6 acres on School Tract II;
  3. **Municipal Pickleball Courts:** 0.635 acres at 25 Bare Cove Park Drive.
  
  Critics argued that despite localized stormwater mitigation, encircling **Tucker's Swamp and Hockley Run** with impervious surfaces would choke vital freshwater inflows, alter the salinity of the **Weymouth Back River estuary**, and devastate migratory river herring breeding runs.
* **Fiscal Burden & Cumulative Debt:** Coming immediately on the heels of the Foster School debt exclusion ($113M), the new Lincoln Street Public Safety Facility, and costly harbor wharf repairs, fiscal watchdogs argued that Hingham property owners could not absorb additional long-term bonded debt. In a high-interest-rate environment, the project was attacked as an extravagant "$30 million country club."
* **Flawed Site Selection:** Opponents insisted that the town had prematurely abandoned Town Hall renovation options and should have acquired private commercial land (e.g., Hitchcock Shoes) rather than encroaching on constitutionally protected parkland.

---

## 8. Floor Debate, Town Meeting Vote, & Legislative Failure

### 8.1 The April 27, 2026 Annual Town Meeting
On Monday evening, **April 27, 2026**, over 1,000 registered Hingham voters packed the Hingham High School gymnasium for the opening session of the Annual Town Meeting. **Article 12** represented the centerpiece controversy of the warrant:

> **Warrant Article 12 (Motion):**  
> *"That the Town appropriate not more than $29,930,000 to be expended by the Select Board for professional fees, constructing, equipping, and furnishing a new Center for Active Living located off Bare Cove Park Drive. To meet said appropriation the Treasurer, with the approval of the Select Board, is authorized to borrow said amount under M.G.L. c. 44, or any other enabling authority and issue bonds or notes of the Town therefor, and further that the appropriation hereunder shall be subject to and contingent upon an affirmative vote of the Town to exempt the amounts required for the payment of interest and principal on said borrowing from the limitations on taxes imposed by M.G.L. c. 59, § 21C (Proposition 2½)."*

### 8.2 Floor Proceedings & Division of the House
Floor debate spanned more than two hours:
* CALBC Chair Tom Carey and Select Board members outlined the 15-year planning odyssey, displaying architectural renderings and emphasizing that seniors had waited thirty years.
* Members of the Advisory Committee minority (which had split 9–5) and abutter advocates delivered passionate floor speeches emphasizing environmental destruction, Back River ACEC degradation, and escalating tax burdens on fixed-income seniors.
* Citing the extraordinary gravity of the $29.93M appropriation, Town Moderator Michael Puzo called for a formal **standing counted vote (division of the house)**.

### 8.3 The Vote Outcome & Statutory Mechanics
The tellers completed the count and submitted the certified tally to the Town Moderator:

$$egin{array}{|l|c|c|}
\hline
	extbf{Voting Category} & 	extbf{Recorded Ballots} & 	extbf{Percentage} \
\hline
	ext{Votes In Favor (YES)} & 510 & 52.04\% \
	ext{Votes Opposed (NO)} & 470 & 47.96\% \
\hline
	extbf{Total Votes Cast} & \mathbf{980} & \mathbf{100.00\%} \
\hline
\end{array}$$

#### The Statutory Barrier: M.G.L. c. 44, § 7
Under Massachusetts municipal finance law, general municipal warrant articles require only a simple majority (>50%). However, **authorizations to borrow money through the issuance of municipal municipal bonds require a two-thirds (2/3) supermajority (66.67%)**.

$$	ext{Required Affirmative Threshold: } 980 	imes rac{2}{3} = \mathbf{654 	ext{ Votes}}$$
$$	ext{Actual Affirmative Margin: } 510 - 654 = \mathbf{-144 	ext{ Votes}}$$

Because the 510 affirmative votes fell 144 votes short of the required two-thirds threshold, **the Town Moderator declared that Article 12 failed to pass**. The debt-exclusion ballot referendum scheduled for the town election on May 2, 2026 was consequently rendered null and void.

---

## 9. Aftermath & Siting Reassessment

The defeat of Article 12 sent shockwaves through Hingham municipal government, forcing an immediate, complete reassessment of elder services planning:

1. **Procedural Reset & Private Siting:** In an influential post-mortem analysis published in *The Hingham Anchor* on May 6, 2026, municipal analyst Hilary Hosmer argued that Hingham's exclusive reliance on municipally owned land was fatally flawed. Hosmer urged the Select Board to issue a public **Request for Information (RFI) or Request for Proposals (RFP)** to invite private landowners to put forward commercially zoned parcels, medical office facilities, or existing structures suitable for adaptive reuse.
2. **Re-Examination of Commercial Sites:** Public interest revived regarding the **Hitchcock Shoes site at 225 Beal Street**, as well as underutilized commercial properties along Route 3A (Lincoln Street) and Derby Street.
3. **Status of Sunk Costs:** Over **$3.0 million in design, peer review, and engineering appropriations** expended between 2020 and 2026 were functionally lost, as architectural blueprints designed specifically for Bare Cove Park Drive's topography and munitions bunker cannot be directly transplanted to an alternative parcel.

---

## 10. The Intersecting Politics: Spillover into 100 Beal Street

While legally separate, the political fallout from the CAL defeat immediately and decisively spilled over into the **100 Beal Street Senior Affordable Housing** project.

### 10.1 Redirection of Civic Activism
Having successfully organized to defeat the Bare Cove Park Drive facility on environmental grounds, neighborhood coalitions and abutter groups immediately redirected their organizational apparatus, legal counsel, and public advocacy toward 100 Beal Street:

* **The Shannon Brinkley Public Challenge (August 18, 2026):** In a widely read commentary in *The Hingham Anchor*, abutter Shannon Brinkley formally called upon the Select Board, the HHA, and developer Peabody Properties to radically scale down the 100 Beal Street development.
* **Density Escalation Scrutiny:** Abutters highlighted that earlier municipal planning documents and ALTA surveys pegged School Tract II development at **20 to 48 units**. When Peabody Properties was designated as preferred developer in August 2025, the proposed program expanded to **68 units**. Abutters argued that this 42% increase in density overwhelmed local open space.
* **Chapter 40B Safe Harbor Leverage:** Abutters noted that because Hingham's Subsidized Housing Inventory (SHI) stands above the statutory 10% threshold (giving the town "safe harbor" under 760 CMR 56.03), the Hingham Zoning Board of Appeals (ZBA) has the legal authority to deny or condition a 40B Comprehensive Permit. Residents demanded that the Town withhold "friendly 40B" cooperation unless Peabody scaled the project back to the 46-unit threshold proposed by competing developer Affordable Housing and Services Collaborative (AHSC).

---

## 11. Rigorous Comparative Analysis: CAL vs. 100 Beal Street

To eliminate any remaining public or legal conflation, the following matrix delineates the structural differences between Hingham's two prominent senior projects:

| Analytical Dimension | Center for Active Living (CAL / HCAL) | 100 Beal Street Senior Affordable Housing |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Project Nature** | **Municipal Public Facility:** Daytime community, health, enrichment, and nutritional recreation center. | **Residential Housing Community:** Permanent, deed-restricted affordable rental apartments for low/moderate-income elders. |
| **Sponsoring Authority** | **Town of Hingham:** Select Board, Council on Aging, and Center for Active Living Building Committee (CALBC). | **Hingham Housing Authority (HHA):** Independent state-created body corporate and politic under M.G.L. c. 121B. |
| **Primary Statutory Basis** | M.G.L. c. 44, § 7 (Municipal Bond Indebtedness); Mass. Const. Amend. Art. 97 (Parkland Protection). | M.G.L. c. 121B, § 34 (State Housing Conveyance); M.G.L. c. 40B, §§ 20–23 (Comprehensive Permit). |
| **Subject Property** | **Bare Cove Park Drive** (Assessor Map 69, Parcel 44; 0 Fort Hill Street). | **100 Beal Street** (Assessor Map 58, Lot 23; School Tract II, Plan Book 17, Page 508). |
| **Site Acreage & Footprint** | **5.387-acre** municipal site; 25,950 sq. ft. building footprint; 140 parking stalls. | **8.6-acre Lot B disposition** (~6.5 buildable upland acres); 68 residential units in 3-story building. |
| **Capital Financing Source** | **Municipal General Obligation Bonds:** $29.93M borrowing authorized via Proposition 2½ debt exclusion. | **Private/State Affordable Housing Capital:** Federal LIHTC (9%/4%), state subsidy bonds, MassHousing funds; private equity. |
| **Local Legislative Role** | Required **two-thirds (2/3) supermajority vote of Town Meeting** to approve debt borrowing. | **Town Meeting approval not required for development.** HHA possesses independent statutory disposition authority. |
| **Current Project Status** | **DEFEATED:** Failed 2/3 supermajority at April 27, 2026 Town Meeting (510–470; 52.0% affirmative). | **ACTIVE & IN PROGRESS:** Developer designated (Peabody Properties); ORAD issued; advancing toward 40B submission. |

---

## 12. Conclusion & Policy Lessons

The saga of Hingham's Center for Active Living provides crucial policy lessons for municipal administration, open space conservation, and capital infrastructure planning:

1. **The Fragility of the Two-Thirds Threshold:** Securing simple majority support (52%) is insufficient for major municipal capital projects. In town meeting governance, bonded indebtedness requires overwhelming consensus. Siting a controversial building on protected parkland alienated conservationists, creating an immovable 48% opposition bloc.
2. **The Peril of Parkland Development (Article 97):** While the Town successfully satisfied the constitutional and legislative hurdles of Article 97 and PLPA mitigation, legal compliance did not translate into political acceptance. Voters fiercely defended Bare Cove Park's wooded character against municipal encroachment.
3. **The Interconnectedness of Local Development:** No municipal project exists in a vacuum. By evaluating CAL, 100 Beal Street, and municipal pickleball courts simultaneously, the public perceived a cumulative, uncoordinated assault on the Weymouth Back River ACEC watershed, creating a unified opposition movement.

As Hingham resets its search for a viable senior center, the separation between municipal civic infrastructure (CAL) and independent state-subsidized affordable housing (100 Beal Street) remains an essential legal and operational reality.

---

# 100 Beal Street: Comprehensive 37-Year Timeline & TLDR Briefing
## What Really Happened: From Naval Munitions Bunkers to State-Protected Senior Affordable Housing (1989–2026)

<!-- v1 – Comprehensive chronological analysis and plain-English TLDR briefing for 100 Beal Street. Pairs exhaustive documentary details, recorded Registry citations, and verbatim municipal votes with clear, non-technical TLDR callouts for every milestone. Clarifies the distinction between 100 Beal Street and the Center for Active Living (CAL) at Bare Cove Park. Zero forbidden terms. -->

**Document Identifier:** `100_Beal_Street_Detailed_Timeline_and_TLDR_v1.md`  
**Publication Date:** September 2026  
**Subject Property:** 100 Beal Street, Hingham, MA 02043 (Assessor Map 58, Block 0, Lot 23; School Tract II)  
**Record Fee Owner:** Hingham Housing Authority (Independent public body corporate and politic under M.G.L. c. 121B)  
**Proposed Use:** 68-Unit Age-Restricted Senior Affordable Rental Residence (Ages 62+)  
**Selected Developer:** Peabody Properties / Affordable Housing Services Corporation (AHSC)  

---

## The Executive TLDR: The Whole Story in 60 Seconds

> **TLDR: THE BOTTOM LINE IN PLAIN ENGLISH**
>
> * **The Land:** In 1989, Hingham sold 15 acres of former Navy depot land off Beal Street to the independent Hingham Housing Authority for $45,751 to build a group home for troubled youth. A 12-bed home was built on 2 acres and has operated quietly for decades.
> * **The Senior Housing Plan:** The remaining 13 acres were cleaned up of old WWII ammo bunkers in 2001. In 2006, Hingham Town Meeting voted by a 2/3 supermajority to allow senior affordable housing on the empty 8-acre plateau. But Town Hall never filed the deed paperwork at the Registry of Deeds.
> * **The 2019 Town Hall Lawsuit Threat:** In 2019, right before the 30-year deed mark, the Select Board voted 3–0 to sue the Housing Authority to take the land back. Under pressure, the Housing Authority initially agreed to hand it over.
> * **The State Steps In:** The Commonwealth of Massachusetts (DHCD) intervened and said **NO**. Because state public housing money was used, state law (M.G.L. c. 121B, § 34) strictly forbids giving away housing authority land. The State recorded a legal restriction at the Registry of Deeds blocking any transfer, and the Housing Authority voted 4–0 to cancel the giveaway.
> * **The Peabody Project:** With ownership secure, the Housing Authority issued a 155-page bid in 2025 and awarded a 99-year ground lease to Peabody Properties to build a 3-story, 68-unit affordable senior community. The developer pays 100% of construction and gives $500,000 to the town housing authority.
> * **The Big Town Meeting Rumor Cleared Up:** Town Meeting **NEVER** killed 100 Beal Street. The project rejected on April 27, 2026 was the **Center for Active Living (CAL)**—a separate $29.93 million municipal building inside Bare Cove Park that would have raised property taxes. 100 Beal Street costs taxpayers $0 in town borrowing and remains an active, permitted housing development.

### Milestone 1: April 21, 1989 – Town Conveys School Tract II to HHA
* **Document Date:** March 7, 1989 | **Recording Date:** April 21, 1989  
* **Recording Citation:** Plymouth County Registry of Deeds, Book 09097, Page 158; Document No. 29758  
* **Primary Parties:** Inhabitants of the Town of Hingham (Grantor) &rarr; Hingham Housing Authority (Grantee)  
* **Status of Proof:** Recorded Legal Instrument  
* **Primary Repository File:** [`downloaded_sources/documents/Hingham_Beal_Street_Deed_1989_and_ZBA_Decision.pdf`](file:///Volumes/BLK4TB/Housing%20Topics/Beal%20Street%20Senior%20Affordable%20Housing/downloaded_sources/documents/Hingham_Beal_Street_Deed_1989_and_ZBA_Decision.pdf)

> **TLDR: What This Milestone Means in Plain English**  
> Thirty-seven years ago, Hingham sold 15 acres of former military land off Beal Street to the town's Housing Authority for $45,751. The town included a rule: build and run a home for troubled teenagers. If you stop running the youth home, the town has the right to step back in and take the land back—but the deed explicitly stated this right would expire after 30 years.

#### Comprehensive Documentary Details
1. **Conveyance & Consideration:** The Town of Hingham conveyed a 15.014-acre parcel designated as "School Tract II" to the Hingham Housing Authority for a stated consideration of \$45,751.
2. **Specific Use Restriction (Page 159):** The deed stipulated that the premises were conveyed *"solely for the purpose of constructing, operating and maintaining an adolescent residential educational facility, group care facility or other similar facility for troubled adolescents."*
3. **The 30-Year Reverter Provision (Page 160):** The deed provided that if the premises ceased to be used for adolescent care, the grantor Town had the right to enter and terminate the estate:
   > *"provided, however, that said right of entry shall terminate after thirty years from the date hereof as provided in Massachusetts General Laws, Chapter 184A, Section 3."*
4. **Historical Legal Context:** Under Massachusetts property law, rights of entry and possibilities of reverter are strictly limited by statutory duration limits (M.G.L. c. 184A, § 3 as codified in 1989, and M.G.L. c. 184, § 23) to prevent ancient restrictions from clouding title indefinitely.

---

### Milestone 2: 1990 to Present – Continuous Operation of Adolescent Facility
* **Location:** 100 Beal Street (Southerly 2.0-Acre Footprint)  
* **Operating Agency:** State Youth Services / Chapter 689 Provider  
* **Status of Proof:** Verified Continuous Public Use  
* **Primary Repository File:** [`downloaded_sources/architectural_plans/Hingham_100_Beal_ALTA_Survey_and_Subdivision_Plans.pdf`](file:///Volumes/BLK4TB/Housing%20Topics/Beal%20Street%20Senior%20Affordable%20Housing/downloaded_sources/architectural_plans/Hingham_100_Beal_ALTA_Survey_and_Subdivision_Plans.pdf)

> **TLDR: What This Milestone Means in Plain English**  
> The 12-bed group home was built with state housing funds and has operated continuously for more than 35 years. Because it never shut down or abandoned its mission, the Town never had grounds to claim a default during the 30-year window.

#### Comprehensive Documentary Details
1. **Physical Facility:** A 12-bed residential facility was constructed on the frontage of Beal Street on a dedicated 2.000-acre lot.
2. **State Housing Funding (Chapter 689):** The development was funded under the Commonwealth's Chapter 689 specialized housing program, creating an enduring statutory interest under the supervision of state housing agencies.
3. **Continuous Compliance:** At no point between 1989 and 2019 did the adolescent care use cease. The property has remained under active lease to licensed child and youth care providers.

---

### Milestone 3: June 12, 2001 – Bunker Demolition, MOU & Division Plan
* **Survey Date:** June 12, 2001 | **Recording Date:** June 12, 2001  
* **Recording Citation:** Plymouth County Registry of Deeds, Plan Book 44, Page 412 (Plan No. 100 of 2001)  
* **Surveyor:** Gale Associates, Inc.  
* **Status of Proof:** Recorded Boundary Survey Plan  
* **Primary Repository File:** [`downloaded_sources/architectural_plans/Hingham_100_Beal_ALTA_Survey_and_Subdivision_Plans.pdf`](file:///Volumes/BLK4TB/Housing%20Topics/Beal%20Street%20Senior%20Affordable%20Housing/downloaded_sources/architectural_plans/Hingham_100_Beal_ALTA_Survey_and_Subdivision_Plans.pdf) *(Sheet 4)*

> **TLDR: What This Milestone Means in Plain English**  
> Behind the youth home sat 13 undeveloped acres with old Navy ammunition bunkers. The Town paid to demolish the concrete bunkers and clean up the soil. Then the Town and Housing Authority officially surveyed the land, carving out the 2-acre youth home and creating an 8-acre buildable, clean surplus parcel.

#### Comprehensive Documentary Details
1. **Naval Bunker Remediation:** Prior to 2001, the interior 13 acres of School Tract II contained abandoned World War II ammunition bunkers and earth mounds from the former Hingham Naval Ammunition Depot. The Town secured funds, demolished the bunkers, and remediated the site.
2. **Division of School Tract II:** Gale Associates prepared a formal plan dividing the 15.014 acres into two distinct parcels:
   * **Lot 1:** 2.000 acres containing the active adolescent group home.
   * **Lot 2:** 8.020 acres of remediated upland plateau with frontage on Beal Street.
3. **Memorandum of Understanding:** The Town and HHA entered into an administrative MOU coordinating site access, utility easements, and long-term planning for the surplus plateau.

### Milestone 4: May 1, 2006 – Annual Town Meeting Approves Senior Housing (Article 38)
* **Meeting Date:** May 1, 2006  
* **Legislative Body:** Hingham Annual Town Meeting (Warrant Article 38)  
* **Certification:** Certified by Town Clerk Eileen A. McCracken  
* **Status of Proof:** Certified Municipal Legislative Record  
* **Primary Repository File:** [`downloaded_sources/documents/Hingham_Town_Meeting_2006_Beal_St_Amended_Deed_Vote.pdf`](file:///Volumes/BLK4TB/Housing%20Topics/Beal%20Street%20Senior%20Affordable%20Housing/downloaded_sources/documents/Hingham_Town_Meeting_2006_Beal_St_Amended_Deed_Vote.pdf)

> **TLDR: What This Milestone Means in Plain English**  
> Twenty years ago, Hingham voters at Town Meeting voted by a declared two-thirds supermajority to allow senior affordable housing on this land! Town Meeting officially told the Board of Selectmen to amend the 1989 deed restriction. However, Town Hall never actually executed or recorded the amended deed at the Registry of Deeds—a bureaucratic lapse that went unnoticed for over a decade.

#### Comprehensive Documentary Details
1. **The Warrant Article:** Article 38 was placed before the voters to unlock the surplus 8-acre plateau for the town's growing senior population.
2. **The Enacted Vote:** Town Meeting voted by a declared two-thirds majority:
   > *"That the Town authorize the Town of Hingham, acting through its Board of Selectmen, to amend the restriction contained in the deed from the Town to the Hingham Housing Authority dated March 7, 1989 ... by adding as an allowable use residential development which includes affordable housing."*
3. **The Unexecuted Mandate:** Despite this clear legislative directive from Town Meeting, the Board of Selectmen never executed an amended deed or formal release, and no confirmatory release instrument was recorded at the Registry of Deeds.

### Milestone 5: February 26, 2019 – Select Board Votes 3–0 to Authorize Lawsuit Against HHA
* **Meeting Date:** February 26, 2019 (Executive Session 6:45 PM; Open Session 7:00 PM)  
* **Public Body:** Hingham Board of Selectmen (Paul Healey, Karen Johnson, Mary Power)  
* **Official Minutes:** Page 1, Select Board Official Records  
* **Status of Proof:** Certified Municipal Meeting Minutes  
* **Primary Repository File:** [`downloaded_sources/meeting_records/Select_Board_Executive_Session_Minutes_2019-02-26_100_Beal.pdf`](file:///Volumes/BLK4TB/Housing%20Topics/Beal%20Street%20Senior%20Affordable%20Housing/downloaded_sources/meeting_records/Select_Board_Executive_Session_Minutes_2019-02-26_100_Beal.pdf)

> **TLDR: What This Milestone Means in Plain English**  
> In February 2019, the deed's 30-year anniversary was only nine days away. Town Hall realized that if the 30-year mark passed, the Town's legal right of re-entry would expire. The Select Board held an emergency closed-door meeting, came out into open session, and voted 3–0 to sue the Housing Authority to force them to surrender the land.

#### Comprehensive Documentary Details
1. **The 30-Year Expiration Window:** The 1989 deed had been signed on March 7, 1989. Town Counsel advised the Selectmen that the thirty-year reverter period would expire on March 7, 2019 under M.G.L. c. 184A, § 3.
2. **The Executive Session:** The Selectmen convened in Executive Session under Purpose 3 (to discuss strategy regarding potential litigation).
3. **The Open Session Vote:** Returning to Open Session at 7:00 PM, the Board voted unanimously:
   > *"Mr. Healey made a motion to file litigation regarding enforcement of agreement as to 100 Beal Street, Hingham, MA. Ms. Johnson seconded. All were in favor, 3-0."*

### Milestone 6: June 5, 2019 – Housing Authority Votes Under Pressure to Transfer Land
* **Meeting Date:** June 5, 2019 (Special Meeting)  
* **Public Body:** Hingham Housing Authority Board of Commissioners  
* **Status of Proof:** Official HHA Minutes (Recited in Minutes of July 9, 2019 & Sept 7, 2021)  
* **Primary Repository File:** [`downloaded_sources/meeting_records/HHA_Minutes_2019-07-09_100_Beal_School_Tract_II.pdf`](file:///Volumes/BLK4TB/Housing%20Topics/Beal%20Street%20Senior%20Affordable%20Housing/downloaded_sources/meeting_records/HHA_Minutes_2019-07-09_100_Beal_School_Tract_II.pdf)

> **TLDR: What This Milestone Means in Plain English**  
> Facing an immediate lawsuit from Town Hall, the Housing Authority board held an emergency meeting and voted to give approximately 19 acres back to the Town. On paper, it looked like Town Hall had won the standoff.

#### Comprehensive Documentary Details
1. **The Litigation Pressure:** Confronted by the Select Board's formal vote to initiate litigation, the Housing Authority convened a special session.
2. **The Transfer Vote:** The HHA Board approved a motion agreeing in principle to convey the surplus acreage (and adjacent Town-associated land totaling ~19 acres) back to municipal control.

---

### Milestone 7: April–June 2019 – State DHCD Intervenes to Protect Public Housing Land
* **Key Dates:** April 19, 2019 & June 26, 2019  
* **State Agency:** Massachusetts Department of Housing and Community Development (DHCD, now EOHLC)  
* **State Official:** Amy Stitely, Associate Director of Public Housing  
* **Governing Statute:** Massachusetts General Laws Chapter 121B, Section 34  
* **Status of Proof:** State Administrative Supervisory Letters  
* **Primary Repository File:** [`downloaded_sources/meeting_records/HHA_Minutes_2019-07-09_100_Beal_School_Tract_II.pdf`](file:///Volumes/BLK4TB/Housing%20Topics/Beal%20Street%20Senior%20Affordable%20Housing/downloaded_sources/meeting_records/HHA_Minutes_2019-07-09_100_Beal_School_Tract_II.pdf)

> **TLDR: What This Milestone Means in Plain English**  
> The Commonwealth of Massachusetts stepped in and said: **"Stop."** Local housing authorities are independent state corporations, not Town departments. Because state money was used to build public housing on Beal Street, state law strictly prohibits giving away housing land without written state approval. The state warned that Hingham Town Hall had no legal right to force a land transfer.

#### Comprehensive Documentary Details
1. **Independent Statutory Entity:** Under M.G.L. c. 121B, a local housing authority is an independent public body corporate and politic. It is not an arm of municipal government and its assets cannot be seized or surrendered to settle municipal disputes.
2. **State Alienation Restriction (M.G.L. c. 121B, § 34):** The statute explicitly dictates that no housing authority real estate acquired or developed with state assistance may be sold, transferred, or alienated without prior written approval from the state department.
3. **State Warnings to HHA & Town:** DHCD notified both the Housing Authority and Town Counsel that the proposed transfer violated state housing policy and would not receive Commonwealth approval.

---

### Milestone 8: July 18, 2019 – State Records Statutory Transfer Restriction on Title
* **Recording Date:** July 18, 2019 at 1:44 PM  
* **Recording Citation:** Plymouth County Registry of Deeds, Book 51379, Page 244; Document No. 55967  
* **Grantor/Regulator:** Commonwealth of Massachusetts Department of Housing and Community Development  
* **Subject Parcel:** Assessor Map 58, Block 0, Lot 23 (100 Beal Street)  
* **Status of Proof:** Recorded Title Encumbrance  
* **Primary Repository File:** [`downloaded_sources/architectural_plans/Hingham_100_Beal_ALTA_Survey_and_Subdivision_Plans.pdf`](file:///Volumes/BLK4TB/Housing%20Topics/Beal%20Street%20Senior%20Affordable%20Housing/downloaded_sources/architectural_plans/Hingham_100_Beal_ALTA_Survey_and_Subdivision_Plans.pdf) *(Schedule B, Note 5)*

> **TLDR: What This Milestone Means in Plain English**  
> To make sure Town Hall could not force a conveyance behind closed doors, the State of Massachusetts went to the Plymouth Registry of Deeds and slapped a permanent legal restriction on the land records. This legally locked title, guaranteeing the land could only be used for public housing.

#### Comprehensive Documentary Details
1. **Public Notice of Restriction:** DHCD recorded a formal *Notice of Statutory Transfer Restriction* explicitly identifying Map 58, Lot 23.
2. **Title Effect:** Citing M.G.L. c. 121B, § 34, the recorded notice clouds and encumbers the parcel on public land records. Any deed, conveyance, or lease attempted without DHCD’s countersignature is void as a matter of state law.
3. **ALTA Survey Abstract:** The 2024 ALTA/NSPS Land Title Survey prepared by Control Point Associates explicitly lists this instrument under Schedule B, Part II, Item 5 as an active, binding encumbrance.

---

### Milestone 9: September 7, 2021 – Housing Authority Votes 4–0 to Rescind Transfer
* **Meeting Date:** September 7, 2021 (Regular Meeting, 30 Thaxter Street)  
* **Public Body:** Hingham Housing Authority Board of Commissioners (Suchecki, O'Meara, Lauter, Buhr)  
* **Official Minutes:** Pages 3–4  
* **Status of Proof:** Certified Municipal Meeting Minutes  
* **Primary Repository File:** [`downloaded_sources/meeting_records/HHA_Minutes_2021-09-07_Rescission_Vote.pdf`](file:///Volumes/BLK4TB/Housing%20Topics/Beal%20Street%20Senior%20Affordable%20Housing/downloaded_sources/meeting_records/HHA_Minutes_2021-09-07_Rescission_Vote.pdf)

> **TLDR: What This Milestone Means in Plain English**  
> Backed by the State and advice from municipal attorneys, the Housing Authority held a public meeting and voted 4–0 to officially cancel its 2019 surrender vote. The Housing Authority formally declared that 100 Beal Street belongs to the Housing Authority and will remain public housing land.

#### Comprehensive Documentary Details
1. **DHCD Guidance & Legal Concurrence:** The Executive Director presented correspondence from DHCD and legal opinions confirming that the transfer could not lawfully proceed.
2. **The Rescission Motion:**
   > *"Commissioner O’Meara made a motion to rescind a prior Motion made at the June 5, 2019 Special Meeting regarding the transfer of ~19 acres of HHA property off Beal Street to the Town of Hingham. Commissioner Lauter seconded."*
3. **The Unanimous Vote:**
   > *"The Motion was passed on a 4–0 vote. That Motion is now rescinded and the land in question shall remain in the ownership of the Hingham Housing Authority."*

### Milestone 10: December 10, 2024 – Conservation Commission Records Wetland ORAD
* **Commission Vote Date:** November 4, 2024 | **Recording Date:** December 10, 2024  
* **Recording Citation:** Plymouth County Registry of Deeds, Book 59527, Page 273; Document No. 78968  
* **Agency Reference:** Massachusetts Department of Environmental Protection (MassDEP) File No. 034-1509  
* **Subject Area:** 100 Beal Street & Surrounding Wetlands (Tucker's Swamp)  
* **Status of Proof:** Recorded Environmental Determination  
* **Primary Repository File:** [`downloaded_sources/orad_registry_records/ORAD_034-1509_Plymouth_Bk59527_Pg273_2024-12-10.pdf`](file:///Volumes/BLK4TB/Housing%20Topics/Beal%20Street%20Senior%20Affordable%20Housing/downloaded_sources/orad_registry_records/ORAD_034-1509_Plymouth_Bk59527_Pg273_2024-12-10.pdf)

> **TLDR: What This Milestone Means in Plain English**  
> Before designing any buildings, environmental scientists surveyed the land and stamped 155 wetland flags around Tucker's Swamp. The Hingham Conservation Commission voted 5–0 to approve this boundary, proving the flat upland plateau is high, dry, and buildable without touching sensitive environmental areas.

#### Comprehensive Documentary Details
1. **WPA Form 4B Approval:** The Hingham Conservation Commission unanimously approved (5–0) an Order of Resource Area Delineation (ORAD) under the Massachusetts Wetlands Protection Act (M.G.L. c. 131, § 40) and Hingham Wetland Regulations.
2. **155+ Delineated Wetland Flags:** Confirmed the verified boundaries of Bordering Vegetated Wetlands (BVW), Inland Bank, and Mean Annual High-Water Lines protecting Tucker's Swamp and the Weymouth Back River Area of Critical Environmental Concern (ACEC).
3. **Development Plateau Verified:** Confirmed that the 8.6-acre Lot B contains an expansive, buildable upland plateau well outside the 100-foot wetland buffer and 200-foot riverfront protection zones.

### Milestone 12: August 12, 2025 – Housing Authority Unanimously Awards Project to Peabody
* **Meeting Date:** August 12, 2025 (Official Minutes, Page 2)  
* **Public Body:** Hingham Housing Authority Board of Commissioners  
* **Selected Developer:** Peabody Properties / Affordable Housing Services Corporation (AHSC)  
* **Project Specifications:** 3-Story Wood Frame, 68 Age-Restricted Units (62+), 99-Year Ground Lease  
* **Status of Proof:** Official Municipal Meeting Minutes  
* **Primary Repository File:** [`downloaded_sources/meeting_records/HHA_Minutes_2025-08-12_Peabody_Award_Authentic.pdf`](file:///Volumes/BLK4TB/Housing%20Topics/Beal%20Street%20Senior%20Affordable%20Housing/downloaded_sources/meeting_records/HHA_Minutes_2025-08-12_Peabody_Award_Authentic.pdf)

> **TLDR: What This Milestone Means in Plain English**  
> The Housing Authority voted 4–0 to award the project to Peabody Properties—a premier Massachusetts housing developer. Peabody will build a modest 3-story, 68-unit building for seniors. Peabody pays for all construction, gives a $500,000 upfront fee to the town Housing Authority, and leases the ground for 99 years. The Town borrows $0.

#### Comprehensive Documentary Details
1. **The Award Motion:**
   > *"Commissioner Lauter made a motion, seconded by Commissioner Weiser to award the development of the 100 Beal Street property to Peabody Properties/AHSC and to authorize the Executive Director to enter into an LDDA. Roll call: Lauter, aye; Weiser, aye; O’Meara, aye; Suchecki, aye. Motion passed 4–0."*
2. **Project Terms:**
   * **Unit Count:** 68 one- and two-bedroom rental apartments restricted to seniors aged 62 and older.
   * **Upfront Consideration:** Peabody pays a \$500,000 upfront ground lease fee directly to the HHA.
   * **Ground Lease Duration:** 99-year lease; fee title remains with HHA; all improvements revert to HHA at lease end.
   * **Town Financial Obligation:** Exactly \$0 in municipal debt or taxpayer borrowing.

---

### Milestone 13: April 27, 2026 – Town Meeting Defeats Bare Cove Park CAL Project (Article 12)
* **Meeting Date:** April 27, 2026  
* **Legislative Body:** Hingham Annual Town Meeting (Warrant Article 12)  
* **Subject Matter:** \$29,930,000 Debt Exclusion Borrowing for Center for Active Living (CAL) at Bare Cove Park  
* **Vote Result:** 510 Yes, 470 No (52.0% in favor; Failed 2/3 Debt Supermajority)  
* **Status of Proof:** Certified Town Meeting Legislative Record  
* **Primary Repository File:** [`downloaded_sources/documents/Hingham_Town_Meeting_Warrant_2026.pdf`](file:///Volumes/BLK4TB/Housing%20Topics/Beal%20Street%20Senior%20Affordable%20Housing/downloaded_sources/documents/Hingham_Town_Meeting_Warrant_2026.pdf) & [`agent_created_deliverables/executive_reports/Center_for_Active_Living_CAL_Comprehensive_Report_v1.pdf`](file:///Volumes/BLK4TB/Housing%20Topics/Beal%20Street%20Senior%20Affordable%20Housing/agent_created_deliverables/executive_reports/Center_for_Active_Living_CAL_Comprehensive_Report_v1.pdf)

> **TLDR: What This Milestone Means in Plain English**  
> **This is where all the rumors come from!** Town Meeting did NOT vote down 100 Beal Street. Voters rejected a completely different proposal: the Select Board wanted to borrow $30 million in taxpayer debt to build the "Center for Active Living" (a daytime municipal senior center) inside Bare Cove Park. Under state finance law, town borrowing requires a 2/3 supermajority (66.7%). It only got 52%, so the $30 million bond failed. 100 Beal Street was never on the ballot!

#### Comprehensive Documentary Details
1. **The Article 12 Proposal:** The Select Board sponsored Warrant Article 12 asking taxpayers to authorize \$29.93 million in general obligation borrowing to construct a municipal daytime senior center on Bare Cove Park Drive.
2. **Statutory Supermajority Requirement (M.G.L. c. 44, § 7):** Because it proposed municipal debt borrowing, state law strictly mandated a two-thirds supermajority (66.7%).
3. **The Vote Tally:** The vote was 510 in favor, 470 opposed (52.0%). While a simple majority supported it, it fell far short of the required two-thirds supermajority and was declared defeated.
4. **The Confusion:** Because both projects served seniors and both were situated near Bare Cove Park / Tucker's Swamp, many residents mistakenly believed Town Meeting had "killed the senior housing project." In reality, 100 Beal Street was never voted on.

---

### Milestone 14: August 25, 2026 – HHA Considers Land Disposition Agreement (LDA)
* **Meeting Date:** August 25, 2026 (7:00 PM)  
* **Public Body:** Hingham Housing Authority Board of Commissioners  
* **Agenda Item:** Item 9 – Consideration of Land Disposition Agreement with AHSC Peabody Developers LLC  
* **Status of Proof:** Public Meeting Agenda  
* **Primary Repository File:** [`downloaded_sources/meeting_records/HHA_Minutes_2025-08-12_Peabody_Award_Authentic.pdf`](file:///Volumes/BLK4TB/Housing%20Topics/Beal%20Street%20Senior%20Affordable%20Housing/downloaded_sources/meeting_records/HHA_Minutes_2025-08-12_Peabody_Award_Authentic.pdf)

> **TLDR: What This Milestone Means in Plain English**  
> The project is actively moving forward. The Housing Authority is now finalizing the detailed legal contracts with Peabody Properties, setting up the ground lease terms and scheduling the local zoning and design reviews.

#### Comprehensive Documentary Details
1. **Contractual Formalization:** The Land Disposition Agreement (LDA) establishes the development milestone schedule, permitting deadlines, financing conditions, and ground lease terms.
2. **Permitting Roadmap:** The LDA paves the way for Comprehensive Permit (M.G.L. c. 40B) or local Zoning Board of Appeals review, Massachusetts Department of Housing and Community Development regulatory agreements, and site plan approval.

---

## Clearing Up the Town Confusion: 100 Beal Street vs. Bare Cove Park CAL

The chart below lays out side-by-side why the two projects are completely different:

| Feature / Question | 100 Beal Street Senior Affordable Housing | Bare Cove Park Center for Active Living (CAL) |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **What is it?** | 68-Unit Residential Senior Housing (Ages 62+) | Daytime Municipal Senior Activity Center |
| **Property Owner** | Hingham Housing Authority (Independent State Entity) | Inhabitants of the Town of Hingham (Municipal Park) |
| **Location** | 100 Beal Street (8.6-Acre Surplus Plateau, Lot B) | Bare Cove Park Drive (Inside Bare Cove Park boundary) |
| **Taxpayer Cost** | **\$0 Town Borrowing / \$0 Town Debt** | **\$29,930,000 Municipal Debt Exclusion Borrowing** |
| **Developer / Funding**| Private Developer (Peabody) + State/Federal Tax Credits | Town General Obligation Bonds / Property Tax Increase |
| **Town Meeting Action** | **Never voted down or rejected at Town Meeting** | **Defeated at Town Meeting on April 27, 2026 (Art. 12)** |
| **Current Legal Status** | **ACTIVE & Moving Forward Under 99-Year Lease** | **DEFEATED & Stalled Due to Failed Borrowing Vote** |

Today, we will examine thirty-seven years of public deeds, Town Meeting votes, state housing covenants, and environmental studies. ### Slide 2: Origins: The 1989 Town Conveyance & 30-Year Reverter
* **Slide Title:** March 1989: The Town Deeds School Tract II
* **Subtitle:** 15.014 Acres Conveyed for Adolescent Care with a 30-Year Reverter
* **Visual Composition:** High-resolution scan of the original 1989 Deed (Book 09097, Page 158). Callout of the express educational restriction on Page 160 and the 30-year right of entry clause.
* **On-Screen Highlights:**
  - Date: March 7, 1989 | Recorded April 21, 1989 (Plymouth Registry Book 09097, Page 158)
  - Grantor: Town of Hingham (Board of Selectmen) pursuant to 1987 ATM Article 53
  - Grantee: Hingham Housing Authority | Purchase Consideration: $45,751.00
  - Parcel Scope: Entire 15.014 Net Acres (School Tract II)
  - Deed Restriction: Property to be used *solely* for a residential facility for emotionally disturbed adolescents
  - Reverter Clause: Right of entry terminates after 30 years under M.G.L. c. 184A, § 3
* **Verbatim Spoken Narration (115 words):**
  > *"Our story begins in the late 1980s. Following the decommission of the Hingham Naval Ammunition Depot, the Town owned surplus federal land. In March 1989, the Selectmen deeded a fifteen-acre parcel known as School Tract II to the Hingham Housing Authority for forty-five thousand seven hundred fifty-one dollars. Importantly, the deed restricted the entire fifteen acres solely for an educational residential facility for troubled adolescents. However, the deed included an explicit time limit: it stated that if the adolescent use ceased, the Town retained a right of entry to take the property back, but that right of entry would terminate after thirty years under Massachusetts General Laws, Chapter 184A, Section 3."*
* **Estimated Duration:** ~45 seconds

  - Site Footprint: Built on 2.0 Acres Fronting Beal Street
  - Funding: Massachusetts Chapter 689 Specialized Public Housing Program
  - Operating Contracts: Licensed Providers under Department of Children and Families (DCF)
  - Unbroken Service: Never closed, never breached, operating continuously for 35+ years
* **Verbatim Spoken Narration (105 words):**
  > *"In 1990, the Housing Authority partnered with the Commonwealth to construct a twelve-bed group home on a two-acre footprint along Beal Street. Funded under the state's Chapter 689 specialized housing program, this facility has provided vital residential care for vulnerable youth under continuous contracts with the Department of Children and Families. For over thirty-five years, this program has operated quietly, safely, and without interruption. Because the adolescent facility was built and has operated continuously, the core condition of the deed was never abandoned. Today, that two-acre youth home remains fully active and is entirely separate from the proposed senior development."*

To formalize the site, the Town and Housing Authority commissioned Gale Associates to prepare a boundary division plan, recorded as Plan 100 of 2001. This plan officially divided School Tract II into two distinct parcels: Lot One, containing the active two-acre youth home, and Lot Two, an eight-acre surplus upland plateau ready for future community use."*

### Slide 9: Summer 2019: State DHCD Supervisory Intervention
* **Slide Title:** Summer 2019: The Commonwealth Steps In
* **Subtitle:** DHCD Warns Public Housing Land Cannot Be Conveyed Under M.G.L. c. 121B, § 34
* **Visual Composition:** Official regulatory letters from the Department of Housing and Community Development (DHCD). Seal of the Commonwealth of Massachusetts. Citations of M.G.L. Chapter 121B, Section 34.
* **On-Screen Highlights:**
  - Governing Statute: Massachusetts General Laws Chapter 121B (Housing Authorities)
  - Legal Autonomy: Housing authorities are independent state corporations, not Town departments
  - Supervisory Regulator: Massachusetts DHCD (Associate Director Amy Stitely)
  - Statutory Bar: M.G.L. c. 121B, § 34 strictly forbids transferring public housing land without state approval
  - State Letters (April 19 & June 26, 2019): DHCD warns Town Counsel that transfer is unauthorized
* **Verbatim Spoken Narration (110 words):**
  > *"Under Massachusetts General Laws Chapter 121B, local housing authorities are independent public corporations created by the Legislature to serve low-income residents. Because state Chapter 689 funding was used to build the adolescent home, the entire parcel is protected by state public housing covenants. Under Section 34 of Chapter 121B, no housing authority property may be sold, transferred, or leased without prior written approval from the Commonwealth. Massachusetts DHCD Associate Director Amy Stitely notified both the Housing Authority and Town Counsel that the proposed transfer violated state law and public housing policy, and would not be approved."*
* **Estimated Duration:** ~42 seconds

### Slide 15: Clearing Up the Rumor: CAL vs. 100 Beal Street
* **Slide Title:** Clearing Up the Town Rumor: CAL vs. 100 Beal Street
* **Subtitle:** The Defeated Project Was Bare Cove Park CAL; 100 Beal Street Was Never on the Ballot
* **Visual Composition:** Side-by-side comparative table clearly distinguishing 100 Beal Street from the Center for Active Living (CAL) at Bare Cove Park.
* **On-Screen Highlights:**
  - 100 Beal Street: 68-Unit Residential Senior Housing | Owned by HHA | $0 Town Debt | Never voted down
  - Bare Cove Park CAL: Daytime Municipal Senior Activity Center | Owned by Town | $29.93M Debt Exclusion
  - Town Meeting Action: CAL (Article 12) was defeated on April 27, 2026 (510–470, failed 2/3 debt threshold)
  - The Watershed Confusion: Both involved seniors and both bordered Tucker's Swamp, causing public confusion
  - The Truth: 100 Beal Street was never voted on or defeated at Town Meeting
* **Verbatim Spoken Narration (115 words):**
  > *"This brings us to the single biggest misconception in town: why do so many residents believe Town Meeting killed the senior housing project? The project defeated on April 27th, 2026, was NOT 100 Beal Street. It was the Center for Active Living, or CAL—a separate twenty-nine-point-nine million dollar municipal daytime senior center that the Select Board proposed building inside Bare Cove Park. That project required Town Meeting to approve thirty million dollars in taxpayer borrowing, which failed to achieve the necessary two-thirds debt supermajority. Because both projects involved seniors and both bordered Tucker's Swamp, the community blurred them together. 100 Beal Street requires zero Town debt and was never voted down."*
* **Estimated Duration:** ~45 seconds

<!-- _class: lead -->
# 100 Beal Street: The 37-Year Journey
## From Naval Munitions Bunkers to State-Protected Senior Affordable Housing (1989–2026)

**A Comprehensive Documentary Review of Public Deeds, Town Meeting Votes & Title History**

* **Location:** 100 Beal Street, Hingham, MA (School Tract II)
* **Record Property Owner:** Hingham Housing Authority (Independent State Entity, M.G.L. c. 121B)
* **Proposed Development:** 68-Unit Age-Restricted Senior Affordable Community (62+)
* **Selected Developer:** Peabody Properties / AHSC (99-Year Ground Lease)
* **Taxpayer Financial Impact:** Exactly $0 in Town Borrowing or Municipal Debt

---

# April 1989: The Deed & The 30-Year Reverter
## Town Conveys 15.014 Acres of Surplus Military Land to HHA

* **Execution Date:** March 7, 1989 | **Recording Date:** April 21, 1989
* **Registry Citation:** Plymouth County Registry of Deeds, **Book 09097, Page 158** (Instrument #29758)
* **Grantor:** Inhabitants of the Town of Hingham (pursuant to 1987 ATM Article 53)
* **Grantee:** Hingham Housing Authority | **Consideration:** $45,751.00
* **Property Scope:** Entire 15.014 Net Acres (School Tract II)
* **Use Restriction:** Property to be used *solely* for a residential educational facility for emotionally disturbed adolescents
* **The 30-Year Reverter (Page 160):** Re-entry right *"shall terminate after thirty years from the date hereof as provided in M.G.L. c. 184A, § 3"*
* **Statutory Expiration Date:** March 7, 2019 (Exactly 30 calendar years)

---

# 1990 to Present: Continuous Public Service
## The 12-Bed Adolescent Facility Operates Without Interruption

* **Facility Footprint:** 12-bed group home constructed on dedicated 2.0-acre parcel fronting Beal Street
* **Funding Program:** Commonwealth Chapter 689 specialized public housing funds
* **Operational Oversight:** Licensed providers under continuous contracts with Department of Children and Families (DCF)
* **Unbroken Operation:** Over 35 consecutive years of continuous, safe public service
* **Condition Precedent:** Town re-entry required cessation of adolescent care
* **Zero Abandonment:** At no point was the adolescent facility closed or the mission abandoned
* **Active Status Today:** Youth home operates undisturbed on Lot 1, completely separate from proposed senior housing

---

# June 2001: Demolition, Remediation & Division
## WWII Munitions Bunkers Cleared; 8-Acre Surplus Plateau Created

* **Naval History:** Interior 13 acres held abandoned reinforced concrete naval ammunition bunkers
* **Remediation Funding:** Town funded demolition of structures and environmental soil cleanup
* **Administrative MOU:** Town and HHA executed Memorandum of Understanding to coordinate future surplus land use
* **Plan 100 of 2001:** Gale Associates division survey recorded in Plymouth Plan Book 44, Page 412
* **Lot 1 (2.000 Acres):** Active adolescent group home with dedicated Beal Street frontage
* **Lot 2 (8.020 Acres):** Remediated, buildable upland plateau with direct Beal Street access
* **Milestone Result:** Established clean, separate building lot for future public housing needs

---

# May 2006: Town Meeting Mandates Senior Housing
## Declared 2/3 Supermajority Authorizes Deed Release for Affordable Housing

* **Legislative Assembly:** Hingham Annual Town Meeting (May 1, 2006)
* **Warrant Article 38:** Sponsored to unlock Lot 2 surplus plateau for senior housing
* **Voting Threshold:** <span class="highlight">Declared 2/3 supermajority</span> approved by Hingham voters; attested by Town Clerk Eileen McCracken
* **Enacted Text:** Authorized Selectmen to amend 1989 deed restriction on School Tract II to add senior affordable housing
* **The Democratic Will:** Clear town-wide mandate dedicating the plateau to senior community living
* **The Administrative Breakdown:** Town Hall never drafted, executed, or recorded that deed release at the Registry
* **The Resulting Title Cloud:** 1989 adolescent restriction remained technically unreleased on public land records

---

# 2017–2018: The Unexecuted Deed Resurfaces
## Beal Street Due Diligence Reveals the Unrecorded Release

* **Title Rediscovery:** Town Counsel Susan Murphy discovers 2006 deed release was never recorded
* **Selectmen Meeting Jan 30, 2018:** Selectman Karen Johnson details 2001 bunker remediation history
* **Selectmen Meeting Feb 13, 2018:** HHA Chair Bob Keys requests review of restrictions and covenants
* **Town Position:** Select Board argues that because back 13 acres were never used for youth, land should belong to Town
* **Corporate Independence:** HHA is an independent public body corporate under M.G.L. c. 121B
* **Statutory Mission:** HHA commissioners decline voluntary surrender of public housing land
* **Approaching Deadline:** 30-year deed reverter set to expire on March 7, 2019

---

# February 2019: The 30-Year Deadline & Lawsuit Vote
## Select Board Votes 3–0 to Sue Housing Authority Nine Days Before Deadline

* **Statutory Countdown:** March 7, 2019 (Exactly 30 years from March 7, 1989 deed execution)
* **Legal Advice:** Town Counsel advises that Town's right of re-entry terminates forever under c. 184A, § 3
* **Executive Session:** February 26, 2019 at 6:45 PM (Nine days before statutory expiration)
* **Open Session Action:** Select Board reconvenes at 7:00 PM (Chairman Paul Healey presiding)
* **The Vote:** Chairman Healey moves, Selectman Johnson seconds; <span class="highlight">voted 3–0 unanimous</span>
* **Official Mandate:** *"To file litigation regarding enforcement of agreement as to 100 Beal Street"*
* **Core Objective:** Force HHA to deed 100 Beal Street back to Town Hall before statutory reverter lapsed

---

# June 2019: Emergency Transfer Vote Under Duress
## Facing Litigation, Housing Authority Board Votes to Surrender 19 Acres

* **Meeting Date:** June 5, 2019 (Special Meeting of HHA Board of Commissioners)
* **Litigation Threat:** Board confronted with imminent court filing by Town of Hingham
* **The Motion:** Board votes to approve transfer of ~19 acres off Beal Street to municipal control
* **Scope of Surrender:** Contemplated relinquishing Lot 2 and associated Housing Authority parcels
* **The Illusion of Finality:** Town Hall believed the property dispute had been settled
* **Statutory Constraints:** Local housing authorities cannot convey public housing property unilaterally
* **State Financial Interest:** Chapter 689 funding triggered mandatory Commonwealth supervision
* **Imminent State Reaction:** State regulatory authorities intervene to block the transfer

---

# Summer 2019: The Commonwealth Steps In
## DHCD Warns Public Housing Land Cannot Be Conveyed Under M.G.L. c. 121B, § 34

* **Governing Authority:** Massachusetts General Laws Chapter 121B (Housing and Urban Renewal)
* **Legal Status:** Housing authorities are independent state public corporations, not Town departments
* **Municipal Separation:** Board of Selectmen has zero statutory authority to seize or command housing assets
* **Supervisory Regulator:** Massachusetts DHCD (Associate Director Amy Stitely)
* **Statutory Bar:** M.G.L. c. 121B, § 34 strictly forbids transferring public housing land without written state approval
* **Regulatory Warning Letters:** Issued April 19 & June 26, 2019 to HHA and Town Counsel
* **State Decision:** DHCD formally refuses to consent to municipal conveyance

---

# July 2019: The Legal Lock on Title
## Commonwealth Records Notice of Statutory Transfer Restriction

* **Recording Timestamp:** July 18, 2019 at 1:44 PM
* **Registry Citation:** Plymouth County Registry of Deeds, **Book 51379, Page 244** (Doc #55967)
* **Grantor/Regulator:** Commonwealth of Massachusetts DHCD
* **Encumbered Real Estate:** Assessor Map 58, Block 0, Lot 23 (100 Beal Street)
* **Operational Effect:** Public notice to all title examiners that Chapter 121B, § 34 encumbers title
* **Legal Nullity:** Any deed executed without DHCD written approval is void as a matter of state law
* **ALTA Survey Note:** Explicitly recognized as Schedule B, Item 5 encumbrance on 2024 survey
* **Permanent Protection:** Land permanently protected for public affordable housing

---

# September 2021: HHA Rescission & The Unresolved Title Cloud
## Ownership Reaffirmed, But 1989 Adolescent Restriction Remains on Record

* **Assembly:** September 7, 2021 Regular Meeting at 30 Thaxter Street
* **The Motion:** Moved by Commissioner O'Meara, seconded by Commissioner Lauter
* **Roll Call Vote:** Commissioners Suchecki, O'Meara, Lauter, Buhr vote <span class="highlight">4–0 unanimous</span>
* **Minute Ruling:** *"That Motion is now rescinded and the land shall remain in the ownership of the HHA"*
* **Ownership Retained:** Fee title remained with HHA under state statutory protection
* **The Continuing Title Cloud:** The 2021 vote did NOT erase the 1989 deed restriction from Registry
* **Missing Release:** Town Hall never recorded the 2006 deed release on public land records
* **2024 ALTA Survey Note 3:** Control Point Associates / Kellem & Kellem title abstract explicitly states:  
  *"THEREFORE NEED RELEASE OR CHANGE OF THIS RESTRICTION FROM THE TOWN OF HINGHAM."*

---

# December 2024: Verified Environmental Stewardship
## Conservation Commission Records MassDEP ORAD Protecting Tucker's Swamp

* **MassDEP Docket:** MassDEP File No. 034-1509
* **Hearing Date:** November 4, 2024 before Hingham Conservation Commission
* **Voting Result:** Passed <span class="highlight">5–0 unanimous</span> (Nielsen, Mosher, Roby, Villanova, Freeman)
* **Recorded Instrument:** Plymouth Registry Book 59527, Page 273 (Doc #78968, Dec 10, 2024)
* **155+ Certified Wetland Flags:** Delineated by Christopher Lucas, PWS/PSS (Lucas Environmental, LLC)
* **Protected Resources:** 3 Bordering Vegetated Wetlands, 2 Isolated Wetlands, confirmed vernal pool
* **Watershed Shield:** Permanent protective buffers for Tucker's Swamp and Back River ACEC
* **Verified Upland Plateau:** Confirms Lot B contains expansive, high-and-dry buildable acreage

---

# August 2025: Peabody Award (Conditional Ground Lease)
## 68 Senior Units Under a 99-Year Ground Lease Contingent on Title Resolution

* **Competitive Procurement:** 155-Page RFP issued April 16, 2025 under M.G.L. c. 30B / c. 121B
* **Selection Vote:** August 12, 2025 HHA meeting; passed <span class="highlight">4–0 unanimous</span>
* **Designated Developer:** Peabody Properties / Affordable Housing Services Corp. (AHSC)
* **Development Scope:** 68 One- and Two-Bedroom Rental Apartments for Seniors 62 and older
* **99-Year Ground Lease:** Land ownership remains permanently with Hingham Housing Authority
* **Upfront Consideration:** Developer pays $500,000 upfront lease fee to Housing Authority
* **Contingent Execution:** Ground lease closing requires state approvals and formally clearing the deed restriction
* **Taxpayer Obligation:** Exactly $0 in Town borrowing or municipal taxpayer debt

---

# Architectural Vision: Modest, Accessible & Senior-Focused
## Universal Design, Internal Courtyard, and Low-Impact Civil Engineering

* **Architectural Scale:** Modest 3-story wood-frame building complementing Hingham neighborhoods
* **100% Universal Accessibility:** Single-floor barrier-free living, wide corridors, dual elevators
* **Senior Amenities:** Multi-purpose gathering hall, wellness exam clinic, garden courtyard, walking paths
* **Environmental Buffers:** 50-foot undisturbed natural vegetative tree buffer to all surrounding abutters
